@@2765010 <p> Tuesday , September 9 , 2014 , 4:19 PM - Time and time again we hear how the vast majority of climate scientists - 97 per cent of them to be more precise - are in agreement about the facts that the Earth is accumulating more heat these days , the reason for it is the excess carbon dioxide being dumped into our atmosphere , and the ultimate source of all that excess CO2 is fossil fuel burning and deforestation . However , despite that overwhelming consensus , apparently the general public believe that agreement is closer to a 50-50 split . In an effort to close this ' consensus gap ' between the scientists and the public , a new Twitter campaign called ' 97 Hours of Consensus ' has started up this week . <p> Over a total of 97 hours , John Cook , a climate communication researcher who runs the Skeptical Science website , is presenting a " who 's who " of 97 climate scientists from around the world , one representing each percentage point of the actual consensus on the science @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by a quote taken from their works or supplied by them to demonstrate their part in the overall consensus . <p> Here 's just a small representation of the #97Hours caricatures so far , a little over halfway through the event : <p> Kicking off the list is Professor Michael E. Mann , from PennState University , the originator of the now famous Hockey Stick graph that reveals the influence of global warming so well . <p> Matthew England , a climate change researcher at the University of South Wales , leads us through the entire argument , step by step . <p> Prof. Ann Henderson-Sellers , a leading climate scientist from Sydney 's Macquarie University talks about the current focus on any uncertainty that does exist these days , and the effects of the ' skeptical ' community . <p> Dr. Penny Whetton , who researches climate impacts and risks at CSIRO , Australia 's national science agency , outlines just what risks we 're facing by ignoring this problem . <p> Prof. Veerabhadran Ramanathan , a climate researcher at UC - San Diego , emphasizes why it 's important @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Along with this overwhelming consensus of climate scientists on the realities of global warming and the climate change that is resulting from it , there are attempts to derail the conversation by stating that ' science is about evidence , not consensus . ' <p> However , the scientific consensus about climate change is not an opinion poll . It 's not a vote . It 's not about personal feelings or beliefs . This is a consensus of the evidence . The scientists who are part of this 97 per cent consensus have seen the mountain of scientific evidence , from all over the globe , and that evidence has guided their perception of what 's going on . <p> The list above is but a small sample of the full 97 Hours of Consensus that Cooke and the Consensus Project are posting . @@2765131 <h> SECTIONS <h> BIOS <h> Climate change to wipe out habitat for hundreds of birds species <p> A kiteboarder navigates across the waters of Boundary Bay during strong winds as a very large flock of ducks take flight in Delta , B.C. , on Saturday December 1 , 2012 . ( Darryl Dyck / THE CANADIAN PRESS ) <p> The flip side of a one-dollar coin may be the only place most Canadians will be able to see a loon by the end of the century , suggests new research from the Audubon Society . <p> The beloved bird with its haunting ululations is one of 314 species --almost half of North America 's birds -- whose habitat is threatened by climate change , says a report by the environmental organization . <p> " We were all a little bit surprised with just how many species were going to lose more than half their current climate space in such a short space of time , " Gary Langham , the project 's lead investigator , said Tuesday . <p> The society used 30 years of climate data and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to conclude that 126 species could lose more than half their current range by 2050 -- with no chance of moving elsewhere . Another 188 species could lose the same amount of habitat by 2080 , but could be able to make up for part of that by moving north . <p> By then , the common loon -- familiar throughout Canada 's cottage and camping country -- would be largely forced into the Northwest Territories and the northern thirds of Saskatchewan , Manitoba and Ontario to breed . About 25 per cent of its non-breeding range and 44 per cent of its breeding range would remain , the report says . <p> The same fate is likely to befall the white-throated sparrow . Its call of " Oh , sweet Canada Canada Canada " could eventually be heard over only 27 per cent of its original breeding range , the rest shifting far to the north of its current home , the report suggests . <p> The problem for birds is that habitats ca n't keep up with the speed of climate change . <p> Langham points to the bobolink @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2080 , that bird 's favoured climate will probably have moved north to the middle of Canada 's boreal forest . <p> " It either has to figure out a way to make a living in a very different climate space or it has to figure out a way to make a living in the boreal forest , which wo n't move as fast as the birds do , " Langham said . <p> " Birds have wings . Trees do n't . " <p> Langham said he hopes the society 's conclusions will encourage people to do what they can to reduce greenhouse gas emissions . He also points out that the research shows where bird habitat is likeliest to remain stable , giving governments a chance to ensure those places remain hospitable . <p> " There are certain places that do n't change very much for the bird 's climate space . We can now have conversations about , OK , this area 's going to stay stable . Is it protected ? Are we managing it to help the bird that 's going to depend on it ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Canada 's boreal forest , said Jeff Wells , an ornithologist at Cornell University in Ithaca , N.Y . <p> " It 's so important that we have big chunks of intact habitat , " Wells said . <p> " That 's the only way we can have local populations that can adapt . You need lots of them and lots of chances for some of them to do well and others to disappear . <p> " The boreal is still a place where that is clearly possible . " <p> Birds matter , said Langham . <p> " Birds rely on the same things that people do . <p> " We all need the same clean air and clean water . It defies reason to think half the birds could be disrupted in this way and that all the other things in nature wo n't be similarly impacted. "

Activists Bill McKibben , Ellen Gabriel Speak at Concordia <h> Indigenous , Migrant Rights Tie into Climate Justice <p> 350. org founder Bill McKibben addresses the dangers of climate change before a crowd of a few hundred students at Concordia . Photo Brandon Johnston <h> The links between indigenous rights , migrant justice and environmental activism were explored at this academic year 's first major speaking event on Wednesday , when a few hundred students turned up to listen to activists Bill McKibben and Ellen Gabriel in Concordia 's Alumni Auditorium . <p> " We have taken a beautiful world and we have begun the process of really de-creating it , " McKibben told an attentive audience . <p> McKibben is the founder of 350. org , a website devoted to environmental activism on an international scale . <p> The movement takes its name from the fact that many climate scientists argue the concentration of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere should be reduced to 350 parts per million , a " safe " level . <p> The speech was given in promotion of the People 's Climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Summit happening later this month . <p> McKibben outlined his beginnings in activism and explained the importance of action to the crowd . <p> " About 25 years ago , scientists gave us pretty much an unequivocal warning about climate change . ... They told us what was going to happen , and nobody did anything , " he explained . <p> " The problem above all problems is that the fossil fuel industry is the richest industry there ever was , and that 's not hyperbole . Exxon made more money last year than any company in the history of money . And , when you have that kind of money , it buys you a lot more political influence than you actually deserve to have . " <p> Ellen Gabriel , a Mohawk human rights activist and artist , echoed McKibben 's views on the corporate role in environmental decay . <p> " Stephen Harper and whoever the CEOs and the board of directors of TransCanada and Enbridge are , I accuse them of crimes against humanity , " she said . <p> Gabriel cited potential leakage of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extraction as reasons for her accusation . <p> Gabriel said indigenous communities are often those most affected by climate change . " <p> In a similar vein , Shaina Agbayani , a Filipino climate justice activist , encouraged audience members to think of " climate justice as migrant justice and as indigenous solidarity . " <p> Of McKibben 's response to her comment , Lickers said , " he did n't address it , ... he basically said , ' we have enough resources that we can make sure it 's brown people in the front , and we can look like we have a real anti-colonial praxis , a real anti-racist praxis , ' and it 's just not true . @@2765214 <h> Believed to be largest year-to-year increase since 1984 <p> Michel Jarraud of the World Meteorological Organization said that carbon dioxide levels increased more between 2012 and 2013 than during any other year since reliable global records have been kept . ( Martial Trezzini/The Associated Press ) <p> Atmospheric volumes of greenhouse gases hit a record in 2013 as carbon dioxide concentrations grew at the fastest rate since reliable global records began , the World Meteorological Organization said on Tuesday . <p> " We know without any doubt that our climate is changing and our weather is becoming more extreme due to human activities such as the burning of fossil fuels , " said WMO Secretary-General Michel Jarraud in a statement accompanying the WMO 's annual Greenhouse Gas Bulletin . <p> " Past , present and future CO2 emissions will have a cumulative impact on both global warming and ocean acidification . The laws of physics are non-negotiable , " Jarraud said . " We are running out of time . The other main contributor , nitrous oxide , reached 325.9 parts per billion , growing at a rate comparable to the average over the past decade . <p> The world has the knowledge and tools to keep global warming within 2 degrees Celsius , a UN goal set in 2010 , Jarraud said , which would " give our planet a chance and ... our children and grandchildren a future " . <p> Carbon dioxide , the heat-trapping gas blamed for climate change , was at global concentrations of 396 parts per million last year . @@2768112 <h> Study : Climate change impacts half of North American bird species <p> If climate change continues , you can forget about seeing the Baltimore oriole in Maryland , and you can say good-bye to the Lesser Prairie-Chicken and the Eastern Whip-poor-will , says David Yarnold , President and CEO of the National Audubon Society . <p> For the past seven years , scientists at the National Audubon Society have been working on a major study -- The Audubon Report -- and the news is not good . <p> It 's no secret to most birders that prairie grasslands in North America are havens for countless species of birds . This is especially true for Western North Dakota , a state with a combination of native prairie festooned with pothole lakes and orderly squares of farm fields . As the writer Michelle Nijhuis points out , this place provides millions of birds key breeding grounds , including many species of ducks . <p> Wikimedia commons <p> In this place , as with seemingly everywhere else , the landscape is changing . Birds still flock to these grasslands each @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and since 2008 , the area has undergone an energy boom of worldwide proportions , Nijhuis writes . Now , fields are scarred with hundreds of rectangles of bright orange raw dirt , where trailers , storage tanks , and pumpjacks sprout like deadly mushrooms . Every single day , these companies suck up nearly a million barrels of oil through hydraulic fracturing out of the Bakken formation , a shale layer that lies two miles underground . Some 8,000 wells are operating , and in the next 20 to 30 years , an additional 40,000 wells could be drilled or fracked . <p> The scientists studied 588 species , and out of that number , they estimated that 314 species could lose more than half of their current range . What 's especially tragic about this is that 126 out of the 314 species have nowhere to go , The Christian Science Monitor ( CSM ) reports . <p> The Audubon Report notes that this area will become even more important for birds as the planet continues to warm . Some 26 species of grassland birds have breeding ranges that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ birds , North Dakota will increasingly become an oasis of viable habitat with suitable climate conditions , the article reports . Protecting parts of this region may very well mean the difference between survival and extinction for some species . <p> The Lesser Prairie-Chicken faces catastrophic habitat loss due to climate change according to a study conducted by scientists at the Audubon Society . <p> That 's only one bit of bad news coming from Audubon 's lengthy investigation into the projected effects of climate change on bird populations in North America . When taken altogether , the news is very bad indeed . By 2080 , the climate model shows that dozens of bird species across the country may be plummeting to extinction . Birds that are already in trouble are n't the only ones . Some that are common sights now , like the American Avocet and the Yellow-headed Blackbird may be in serious trouble before the end of the century . The Piping Plover , an iconic part of the American Flyway may disappear from most eastern shores , while other birds such as the Chestnut-collared Longspur may @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for the Baird 's Sparrow may vanish completely , per the article . <p> Even birds that are seemingly common are very likely to be adversely affected by climate change . <p> By U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service Headquarters ( yellow-headed blackbird Uploaded by Dolovis ) CC-BY <p> How the study was conducted <p> The scientists used three decades of citizen-scientist observations gleaned from the Audubon Christmas Bird Count and the North American Breeding Bird Survey to determine the " climatic suitability " for each species -- including the range of temperatures , precipitation , and seasonal changes each bird species needs to survive . Next , the scientists used internationally recognized scenarios for greenhouse gas emissions and mapped where each bird 's ideal climatic range may be found in the future as climate change continues . These maps serve as guides to how a bird 's current range could expand , shift , or contract inside three future time periods -- 2020 , 2050 , and 2080 , per the article . <p> Four Audubon Society scientists wrote the report and in it they project that 21.4 percent of existing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climactic range by 2050 , and these birds wo n't have the option of making up losses by moving to other areas . The scientists said that by 2080 an additional 32 percent will be in the same predicament , The New York Times reported . <p> The study spanned across Alaska , Canada , and the lower 48 states . One factor that added a complication is that dozens of North American birds spend the winter in Latin America , and according to the report , the study lacked sufficient data to study the effects of global warming on the winter ranges of these birds . This makes it likelier that even more birds are threatened by global warming than the study was able to identify so far . <p> Now , scientists are identifying priority areas for conservation -- areas that are strongholds where birds currently live and it 's thought that these areas will remain critical for birds even in the midst of climate change . The scientists are also working to add additional data to generate more robust projections , the article reports . <p> Some of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ northern hawk owl , the northern gannet , Baird 's sparrow , the rufous hummingbird and the trumpeter swan , the report said , per the Times . These birds are among the 30 species that , by 2050 , will unable to live and breed in more than 90 percent of their current territory . <p> " Common sense will tell you that with these kinds of findings , it 's hard to believe we wo n't lose some species to extinction , " Yarnold told the Times . " How many ? We honestly do n't know . We do n't know which ones are going to prove heroically resilient . " <p> Plus , some bird species can move , but others ca n't , Yarnold noted . <p> " Some can and some will , " he said . " But what happens to a yellow-billed magpie in California that depends on scrub oak habitat ? What happens as that bird keeps moving higher and higher and farther north and runs out of oak trees ? Trees do n't fly . Birds do . " <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ like what our grandparents experienced , with the birds they had , is unlikely , " Gary Langham , the study 's chief author said in an interview , the Times reports . Impact due to climate change wo n't just harm birds that are already considered endangered -- it 's also just as likely to devastate birds that have healthy populations right now . <p> " This whole other threat tends to undo successes we 've had in the past , " Langham said . <p> Puffins are one example of this . The reintroduction of these colorful birds off the Maine coast had been , by all accounts , a success story for the Audubon Society . Now , however , the little birds are showing signs of decline off the American coast and in countries like Iceland and England . This may be due to changes in the ocean food web due to climate change , the Times reports . <p> " Every species of plant and animal is very well-tuned to what it does for a living , " Langham said . " A bird in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about every organism is finely tuned . " <p> Langham is calling for a revised conservation strategy -- one that focuses on species in immediate trouble and those that are likely to suffer when climate forces them out of the homes they live in now . The Audubon report is " a framework to focus on which species are predicted to be sensitive and where they are likely to move , and importantly , where they are likely not to move , " he said , per the Times . <p> Birds are resilient , Yarnold said , but climate change is definitely going to test just how resilient they are . <p> " We just do n't know whether they 'll be able to find the food sources and the habitat and cope with a new range of predators , " he said , per the Times . " Maybe they 'll all be incredibly hardy and find ways to survive . " <p> " That does n't seem likely , given , one , the number of birds affected , and two , the pace at which these things @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out which birds in your area are likely to be impacted , here 's the link . <p> If we act quickly , perhaps we can stem the loss of so many beautiful birds , but as the study shows -- time is of the essence .

( JASON FRANSON / CP ) <p> Jim Prentice , who became Alberta 's premier-designate by virtue of a first-ballot Conservative leadership victory this weekend , has more history with Stephen Harper than any of his past and present provincial colleagues . <p> A front-line cabinet player in Harper 's two minority governments , the incoming premier has an insider 's perspective on what makes the prime minister tick . <p> Precious few people in positions of power and influence in Canada can boast as much . <p> But whether that will make for an easygoing relationship between the two governments is questionable . <p> Prentice takes the reins at a time when the Alberta 's energy agenda is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ notion that it will not be advanced without a recast on climate change and a thaw in the usually frosty relationship between the government-backed industry and First Nations . <p> As a former federal aboriginal affairs and environment minister , Prentice has the credentials to effect part of that recast . But he ca n't do it alone , in the face of a federal determination to stay the dubious course set over the past decade . <p> Brian Mulroney more than hinted as much in various interviews last week . And while he was speaking as a former prime minister , his conclusions were also informed by his more recent experience as a global business player . <p> Better than anyone outside of Harper 's circle , Prentice has a take on the limits of the change in tone he can hope for on the current federal watch . <p> From the outside it is far from clear that Alberta 's economic interests -- inasmuch as they rest on unclipping the export wings of its energy sector -- would be best served by the re-election of a Harper-led government next @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's new role than leading a province that no federal leader can afford to ignore . <p> Prentice who , in another life , ran to succeed Joe Clark as Progressive Conservative party leader is now the highest-ranking red Tory in office in the country . <p> As provincial party leader , Prentice needs to stay on the good side of his former political boss , because job one for the Alberta Tories involves repairing the battered reputation of their mid-mandate government . <p> Over the last few years , Christie Clark in British Columbia , Kathleen Wynne in Ontario and Philippe Couillard in Quebec have all demonstrated that a fresh face can work wonders on a tired brand . <p> But what is different in Prentice 's predicament is that the biggest threat to the survival of the provincial dynasty he has inherited and the unity of his government comes not from across the political aisle but from within the larger Conservative tent . <p> In the battle against the Wildrose Party , Prentice can expect little more from Harper than neutrality . Even if the prime minister were so @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his will on his Alberta caucus . <p> That being said , it is equally in Harper 's interest to stay in Prentice 's good books . Harper ca n't win a fourth mandate and a second governing majority next year without winning back the middle-of-the road voters he has lost since the last election . <p> He ca n't afford to have the red Tories sit on their hands across the country as he fights for re-election next year or to further alienate them between now and then . <p> Ralph Klein was Alberta 's last transformative premier . But one has to go back further , to Peter Lougheed and the beginning of the province 's Tory dynasty , to find a premier with as outward-looking a perspective as Prentice 's . <p> Klein inspired Ontario 's Conservative " Common Sense Revolution " and he led the country by example on the deficit-fighting front , but the national scene was never his forte . <p> Even Lucien Bouchard -- a sovereigntist premier -- was a happier camper at First Ministers gatherings than Klein. 
@@2768217 <h> France aims to restore dark skies , turning night into night <p> Paris - As this year 's autumnal equinox approaches , hundreds of towns and cities across France will switch off their lights in an event designed to show off the night skies , free of light pollution . <p> French population centres large and small are participating in La Jour de la Nuit -- The Day of the Night -- organized by environmental campaigning group Agir pour l'Environnement . The event is scheduled for the night of Saturday , September 20 , the weekend closest to 2014 's autumnal equinox falling on September 23 . At the equinox , the Sun shines directly overhead on the Equator . In both the northern and southern hemispheres , the days and nights are of equal length . In the northern hemisphere the autumnal equinox marks the start of autumn , while on the other side of the Equator , spring officially gets underway . <p> In 2013 , over 400 events were organized in France to mark La Jour de la Nuit . For 2014 , these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the impact of light pollution . <p> Light pollution not only dilutes the numbers of stars to be observed in night skies but also affects the rhythm of nature , destabilizing ecosystems and impacting on species like deer , bats and moths that prefer the more discreet light of dusk . <p> France , with almost 9 million bright spots , is also guilty of engaging in a colossal waste of energy , according to Agir pour l'Environnement . But , extrapolating that worldwide , night-time energy use is a major contributory factor to global warming and the latter 's impact on the environment . <p> Agir pour l'Environnementrefers to a study from Australia 's University of New South Wales that found global average night-time temperatures showed an average increase of 3 ? C over the last 60 years . That appeared to indicate nights were more affected by climate change than daytime with knock-on effects for night-time biodiversity . Scientists observed a correlation between warmer nights and alterations in the biological cycles of plants and both warm-blooded animals and cold-blooded animals . Warm-blooded animals became more active while their cold-blooded @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to the United Nations Environment Programme ( UNEP ) , lighting accounts for 15 percent of global electricity consumption and 5 percent of greenhouse gas emissions . UNEP 's " en.lighten " initiative aims to encourage nations to introduce concrete measures to move to more efficient lighting , with an ambitious target date of end-2016 set for phasing out all inefficient lighting . <p> If such measures are n't put in place , says UNEP , overall energy consumption for lighting alone would grow by between 60 and 70 percent by 2030 , with massive consequences for climate change . <p> France has already taken some steps to reduce light pollution and , with it , energy consumption . In July 2013 , legislation came into force restricting the hours during which owners of non-residential buildings could use interior lighting or illuminate their shop windows and storefronts . <p> But much more requires to be done , say Agir pour l'Environnement whose figures point to the number of " bright spots " in France having increased by 30 percent during the past decade such that they now number 8.7 million points @ @ @ @ @ @ @

It has cut deforestation in the Amazon by 70 per cent , compared to the average level in 1996-2005 , making zero deforestation by 2020 -- or even sooner -- a feasible goal . <p> Many @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Presidents Lula da Silva and Dilma Rousseff , Environment Minister/Green Party candidate Marina Silva , state governors , independent public prosecutors , NGOs , the voluntary moratoria by the soybean and beef industries , and the results-based financing provided by Norway ( $1 billion ) . <p> The overwhelming role of soy and beef as causes of Amazon deforestation means that successful civil society pressure on these industries ' supply chains could lead to rapid reductions in deforestation . <p> Brazilian civil society , including indigenous groups , rubber tappers , labour unions , and environmentalists , were able to change the political dynamics and framing of the deforestation issue . They forced governments and businesses to act and thus made the success possible . <p> Brazil 's reduction in emissions from deforestation is the largest contribution so far by any country -- rich or poor -- to reducing global warming pollution . @@2770872 <p> But I just ca n't resist offering my two cents on the latest round of jazz squabbling . Here 's the recap : <p> John Halle is the director of studies in music theory and practice at Bard College Conservatory in Hudson , N.Y . He 's also a pianist who gigged decades ago with , among others , Joe Henderson , Freddie Hubbard and Sonny Stitt . That said , he calls himself a " reformed bebopper " on his Twitter page . A few days ago , Halle wrote a piece entitled Jazz After Politics for the leftist publication Jacobin . <p> So far , so dull , right ? It gets more interesting . <p> Taking Justin Moyer 's ill-conceived anti-jazz rant in the Washington Post -- the one that derided jazz as " " boring , " " overrated , " " washed up , " and which I rebutted here -- as his point of departure , Halle makes a number of political and aesthetic assertions , that rankled many a jazz-loving reader . <p> First , Halle told The Jacobin 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ think , does not skew left . Noting " the Left 's long history of embracing jazz and jazz musicians , " Halle says such days are over : " It 's been years since jazz had any claim to a counter-cultural , outsider , adversarial status , or communicated a revolutionary or even mildly reformist mindset . " He cites the corporate sponsorship of jazz ( Jazz at Lincoln Center , the branded jazz club Dizzy 's Club Coca Cola ) as evidence . He adds elsewhere : " My perspective is that it jazz has almost 100 per cent approval , particularly among intellectuals and academics . In all my years in universities , I ca n't remember anyone once disparaging the music -- or even criticizing any aspect of it . " <p> Turning to the music itself , Halle wrote : " Moyer pretty effectively slays one sacred cow implicated in this : not all improvised music is great . Yes , improvisation has produced some wonderful music , but it has also imposed plenty of tedium on audiences over the years . " <p> Growing more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ jazz shows -- at least , of the assembly-line variety I was doing routinely back then -- for reasons that square with some of Moyer 's objections . One was over-reliance on improvisation to inflate generally pretty unremarkable ( and usually unrehearsed ) head arrangements , almost always for one or more horns plus " rhythm section . " A drummer friend described the product as " driving the bus " : the horns , the piano , the bass and/or drums lining up to " blow on the changes . " <p> " The aura of improvisation was a pretense masking an unvarying formula , the stuff of probably tens of thousands of live performances and recordings . The New Yorker critic Whitney Balliett famously described jazz as " the sound of surprise , " but now it tends towards the opposite . " <p> Halle then devotes a few paragraphs to criticizing jazz musicians who fall short because they cover show tunes with bad and even offensive lyrics . Halle 's exhibit A was Joe Henderson performing Without A Song , which Halle considers " the nadir of obliviousness @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) include the word " darky , " andf or many commentators , this material was the lightning rod of Halle 's piece . I wo n't go down the wormhole of defending Henderson and debunking Halle on this specific charge because Bad Plus pianist Ethan Iverson , at his award-winning Do The Math blog , has already done that definitively in two posts , here and here . <p> I will respond , though , to Halle 's other contentions . <p> Regarding jazz and the Left , Halle 's perspective is , if not distorted , severely limited . From what I 've seen , jazz players and left-leaning politics remain naturally connected . Recall the concerts that jazz musicians staged in support of U.S. President Barack Obama 's candidacy in 2008 , the various pieces such as Robin Eubanks ' Yes We Can that celebrated Obama 's victory , or Toronto saxophonist Rich Underhill 's performance for at the 2011 state funeral of NDP leader Jack Layton . Recall the anti-Bush statements of albums such as Charlie Haden 's 2005 album Not In Our Name , the World @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Montreal saxophonist Christine Jensen entitling her 2006 CD Look Left . Now try to name some meaningful right-wing expressions or shows of support by jazz stars . <p> This is not to say that all music is politically charged or needs to be . When jazz musicians show their stripes , they seem to have skewed left ( myself included ) , but at other times , the music is simply apolitical . As Iverson writes , sometimes pieces such as Without A Song " are just good tunes for a improvisor to dig into . " <p> Put it this way , sometimes the jazz musicians on my Facebook feed get political and decry Stephen Harper or climate change , and at other times they simply promote their gigs and post photos of cats . <p> As for Halle 's attempts to knock improvising off its pedestal , I have a few thoughts . <p> First , I can agree with him that bad improvising sucks . Beyond that , I have , I think , more time for the practice of improvising than he does , and for what @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after another playing on chord changes . I do n't think there 's anything inherently wrong with it , provided that it 's done well . It 's all about the inspiration that everyone , soloists and accompanists alike , bring to the " formula " to make the music fresh and exciting . Frankly , the " formula , " whether it 's a familiar song or the marking of musical structure , provide the frame and context for the " sound of surprise , " to cite Whitney Balliett , as Halle does . <p> ( An aside : pondering " the sound of surprise , " Montreal pianist Josh Rager just Facebooked me after I sought elaboration on what Balliett meant by that phrase : " Why do the records I listen to continue thrill me even when I know 100 per cent what 's coming next ? " ) <p> Halle says that jazz -- as if it 's a monolithic art form -- " it tends towards the opposite " of " surprise . " Clearly he does n't get the hundreds of CDs that I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 've heard , there is , first of all , no shortage of new compositions and not just retreads of jazz standards , as well as more formal tweaks and even innovations to vary that formula that Halle regards as tiresome . <p> Let me add that in recent years , I 've seen something of a shift to include more tracks on recordings , both instrumental and vocal , so that improvised solos tend to be more concise and fit within shorter arcs . <p> These points aside , what 's more important than the analytical breakdown of jazz conventions is the ability and the efficacy of the musicians , soloing or otherwise , to connect with listeners . And I just do n't think that most listeners who go to the effort of attending live jazz shows are jaded about head-solo-head formats . <p> The matter of who listens to jazz and likes it arises in passing in Halle 's first post . Jazz , he says , is " catering to audiences of ' jazzbros ' who are temperamentally , intellectually , and , probably , politically a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ age a half century ago . " <p> Ah , you have much to answer for , Nate Chinen . The Jazz Times/New York Times scribe tagged young jazz students as " jazzbros " in a 2013 column . When I read it , my reaction , however , my reaction was " Ha , ha , " not , " Oh ! A blight upon the music ! " and I was surprised to see Halle seriously making a straw man of " jazzbros " when Chinen , I thought , was mostly having a laugh . <p> Now , on his own blog , Halle wrote a follow-up piece to his Jacobin piece , entitled When Jazzbros Attack . It turned out that the reaction to his initial piece included many gutter-language responses via email and comments . Having received a few such low blows myself , I can sympathize . <p> That said , Halle 's estimation of and ire against jazzbros are both curiously overblown . <p> Maybe Halle , in his outings to hear live jazz , has seen sufficient jazzbros in attendance to deem that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them . I do n't see it that way . In considering the dozens of concerts I 've attended this year , at clubs in Ottawa and Montreal and festivals in both cities , I did n't hear anything that resembled music performed for music students , or indeed , those jazzbros whooping like boors that made me long for return of the beatniks . The live jazz experiences I 've had felt more to me like gatherings of open-minded , jazz-positive listeners giving the music at least half a chance to stimulate and move them . <p> Indeed , the two complaints about jazz audiences that I hear and see re-iterated ( and this is apart from there are n't enough bums in seats ) are that the audience tends to be too grey-haired and that the jazz students are n't going out to hear the music . In both cases , it comes down to insufficient younger listeners supporting jazz . If there 's a " death " in sight for jazz , it will happen when younger listeners have completely deserted the music , not when people like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of what he wrote , Halle still calls himself " a jazz fan , who loves the music , warts and all . " I hope this is the case . And yet , it 's hard to discern his love for jazz beyond token affirmations ( Joe Henderson was a " genius , " even if he was oblivious , Halle slips in ) when what he writes is so overwhelmingly negative , targeting jazz practices , jazz greats and jazz listeners . <p> As Moyer 's piece did , Halle 's writing left me bemused about why someone , who presumably loved the music enough to study it deeply , and in Halle 's case , apparently could execute jazz in the company of some of its finest practitioners , would apparently turn into such a wet blanket . But perhaps , despite the sharp arrows I 've launched against music that I thought did n't work , I 'm still part of what Halle calls " the stultifying , cultish atmosphere surrounding jazz , one which craves establishment respectability while dismissing as heretical any failure to wave the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I say that if Halle 's still a jazz admirer , in light of the dissing that I think his piece was , he ought to find it in him , as a matter of balance , to write a companion piece about the jazz of today that he likes . That might smack overly of uncritical advocacy to him , but I think it 's what someone who is n't embittered and weighed down by an ax to grind would do . <h> Comments <p> We encourage all readers to share their views on our articles and blog posts . @@2770953 <p> On Wednesday , Mayor Malcolm Brodie announced his candidacy for the mayoral " race " for the City of Richmond . <p> Standing uncontested to date , Brodie , the city 's mayor since 2001 , could become the second longest-standing mayor/reeve in the history of Richmond should he follow through on the upcoming four-year term , Brodie would tie former mayor Gilbert Blair with 18 years of public service , with only Rudy Grauer , who served as Richmond 's reeve from 1930 to 1949 , having served longer in the city 's top political chair . <p> " I will be formally running for the position of mayor of the City of Richmond in the upcoming civic election , which is to take place on November 15th , " said Brodie at his office in Richmond City Hall . <p> Brodie , a lawyer by profession , left off last election with a political war chest of $164,000 , which has since been kept in a City of Richmond trust fund . I believe we have to continue growing and grow with quality of life in mind , " said Brodie , dismissing the notion that the city has grown too fast . <p> He cited the city 's expected population boom of about 75,000 more people in the next 25 years and said densification will need precision planning , including new community facilities , increased policing , a transportation plan focused on the Canada Line , and parkland development , among other things . <p> Brodie said the city 's goals of addressing climate change and conserving the environment and agricultural land has been " in our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Greenway and the Richmond Olympic Oval as a great success of incorporating active living in the city . Reader Marilyn Cameron argues that preserving the province 's agricultural land is , in the long run , more important than short-term economic benefits from fracking . <p> Congratulations to our government for wanting to place a ban on fracking . Finally , someone here has a vision for the future . Thousands of citizens came out to meetings or wrote letters to say no to shale gas and yes to getting a renewable energy strategy on the table so that we can all reap the benefits of a cleaner and safer environment while long-lasting jobs are created and our energy needs met . All the panic-mongering shale gas supporters want us to believe that fossil fuel-related jobs and economic prosperity need to be placed above finding immediate solutions to climate change . These two industries have the potential to grow and prosper -- and both already contribute more to our economy per year than fracking ever will in sales and jobs created . That money stays in the province because the farmers , tourism operators and workers live here . If the farmland resource and landscapes are not degraded and industrialized , both agriculture and tourism will continue to exist in harmony forever . A fracked well 's production lasts but a fleeting moment compared to the endless bounty of safe and nutritious food from land unfracked . Compare Wheeler 's map outlining where fracking could be sited and the Department of Agriculture 's map showing where our best agricultural land is , and it is easy to see that the industry could destroy half to two-thirds of it -- right along with our food security and our growing buy-local movement . We are having great difficulty managing the wastewater from a couple of fracked wells in Kennetcook . How could we ever manage wastewater from thousands of wells all over N.S. ? <p> The majority of Nova Scotians who simply do not want shale gas fracking , including the rural folks who are in the path of fracking , are being called upon to suck it up and to embrace this industry as our economic saviour and bear the brunt of impacts to our health and happiness for the good of the few who will benefit . Some are very vocal because they have a lot to lose as they would benefit financially from fracking . I have to ask , who really are the ecosystem terrorists ? @@2773761 <h> External Links <p> ( Note : CBC does not endorse and is not responsible for the content of external links . ) <p> Aerial photographs show different aspects of the final stages of the collapse of the Larsen B ice shelf in 2002 : ( clockwise from top left ) the shelf breaking up near Foca Nunatak , a rift in the ice sheet near Cape Desengano , a cascade of water from melting ice nearly 30 meters high along the front of the shelf , and the new front edge of the shelf breaking up near Cape Foyn . ( STR New/Reuters ) <p> Warmer air triggered the collapse of a huge ice shelf off Antarctica in 2002 , according to a new report that may help scientists predict future break-ups around the frozen continent . <p> Antarctica is a key to sea level rise , which threatens coastal areas around the world .. It has enough ice to raise seas by 57 metres if it ever all melted , meaning that even a tiny thaw at the fringes is a concern . <p> Until now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ice shelf , a floating mass of ice bigger than Luxembourg at the end of glaciers in the Antarctic Peninsula , had been unknown . Some experts suggested it was thinned by sea water from below . <p> Writing in the journal Science this week , a team of scientists blamed rising air temperatures , saying that melt water and rain in the brief Antarctic summer had flowed into deep cracks . <p> Water expands when it turns to ice , and the re-freezing meltwater in the Larsen-B shelf -- perhaps 200 metres thick -- led to a build-up of huge pressures that shattered the ice in 2002 . <p> The Larsen B Ice Shelf in April 2006 . ( Michele Rebesco ) <p> A rival theory had been that warmer sea water had destabilized ice where the shelf was grounded on the seabed . Studying the seabed , however , the scientists found evidence that water had flowed freely under the ice for the past 12,000 years . <p> " This implies that the 2002 Larsen-B Ice Shelf collapse likely was a response to surface warming , " they wrote @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ around the Antarctic Peninsula , which is below South America . <h> Warning sign <p> The Larsen-B captured the public imagination and even featured in a Hollywood disaster movie about climate change , The Day After Tomorrow , showing a huge crevasse appearing through a scientific base on the ice . <p> Researchers collected seismic data from U.S. research icebreaker Nathaniel B. Palmer in April 2006 in the area of the former Larsen B Ice Shelf as part of the study . ( Michele Rebesco ) <p> " Hollywood underplayed that one , " said Eugene Domack , an author of the study at the University of South Florida . " It fractured into thousands of icebergs , not just one huge crevasse . " <p> Loss of floating ice shelves does not directly affect sea levels but can accelerate the slide of glaciers from land into the sea , raising levels . @@2773890 <p> In 10:04 , Ben Lerner 's beautiful new novel , it is always " unseasonably warm . " The world " rearranges itself " -- around the narrator , when a mentor falls ill ; around a woman who learns her father is not her biological parent . The planet rearranges itself more literally , too , growing hotter and menacing New York with apocalyptic storms . Everything is " unusual " -- Whole Foods shoppers are " unusually polite " as an " unusually large cyclonic system " approaches the city -- and reality is constantly " flickering . " The book 's epigraph tells of a Hasidic story in which " the world to come " is almost identical to our own , but not quite . That refrain returns again and again . " Everything will be as it is now , " the leitmotif goes , " just a little different . " <p> Lerner 's first novel , Leaving the Atocha Station , also employed such refrains ; the most crucial was " a profound experience of art , " or , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . ( There were also , thrillingly , small glimpses at what would become the major themes and symbols of 10:04 : " unseasonably " warm or cool weather ; rising sea levels ; alienation from the familiar , and from oneself ; " Everything was a little changed , a little charged . " ) Published by a small press in 2011 , Leaving the Atocha Station was a surprise critical smash , and much was said about its apparently autobiographical nature . The narrator was , like Lerner , an American poet from Topeka on a prestigious fellowship in Spain , and the book felt deeply , painfully experienced . <p> Much of the same will be said about 10:04 . The unnamed narrator is a poet whose previous novel was unexpectedly successful ; he shares the anxieties and self-loathing of said previous novel 's narrator ; and so on . Crucially , however , 10:04 pre-empts questions about what is and is n't autobiographical , or " real , " by making those questions the object of its study . This is a stupid way to put it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Or , rather , it is about perception : about " the tension between the metaphysical and physical worlds , " and " the realistic fiction the world appears to be . " <p> Part of what made Leaving the Atocha Station so affecting was the slow , rolling rhythm of its prose , the clauses and semicolons and commas unspooling themselves endlessly , each sentence spiraling around , but never quite reaching , something that resembled meaning ; this was coupled with the narrator 's refusal to accept that he understood Spanish . ( " She described the death of her father when she was a little girl , or how the death of her father turns her back into a little girl whenever she thinks of it ; he had been young when he died but seemed old to her now , or he had been old when he died but in her memories grew younger . " ) It was a funny book , but in a bleak way , as when the narrator , inexplicably and untruthfully , tells a love interest that his mother is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ respects violence , " then thinks of his dad " patiently trying to get a spider from the carpet onto a piece of paper so he could escort it safely from house to yard . " <p> 10:04 is different . Line for line , it is written more accessibly than its predecessor , and is more straightforwardly uproarious ; there is , for example , a lewd scene in a fertility clinic , in which the narrator is rendered helpless by his inability to select the pornography to which he wants to masturbate . The book more or less has a plot : a writer agrees to father his best friend 's child , panics about a possibly life-threatening condition , lives through Hurricane Irene and Superstorm Sandy , and struggles to write his sophomore novel . If this is a more traditional book than Leaving the Atocha Station , though , we are made to believe that it is deliberately so -- not because the narrator wants to write something more traditional , but because his metafictional project demands it . " Well , your first book was unconventional @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ during an opulent lunch to celebrate the " strong six-figure " advance he 's received for his new novel . " What they 're buying when they buy the proposal is in part the idea that your next book is going to be a little more ... mainstream . " <p> Lerner both has and has not delivered on that expectation . Certainly , the prose is less lyrical , the one-liners more pointed ( a pair of authors are " so distinguished I 'd often thought of them as dead " ; a real-life kiss is " the sexiest kiss in the history of independent film " ) . It 's a powerful , compelling book , with a straightforward emotional heft that , in Lerner 's previous novel , was more lightly sketched . But 10:04 is , structurally and formally , much weirder than Leaving the Atocha Station . For example , the second chapter is a short story Lerner published in The New Yorker in 2012 , The Golden Vanity ; it is presented as the short story itself , with its title in a New Yorker-esque @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not appear anywhere else in the book , denoting section breaks . The narrator received his novel 's advance on the strength of The Golden Vanity , and later , when his agent tells him " you need to keep the New Yorker story in there , " we understand why it appears in the form it does ; no one can say that the New Yorker story is not " in there . " <p> Similarly , a version of a self-published book about dinosaurs , which , according to the acknowledgements , Lerner wrote with a child named Elias Garcia ( who in 10:04 becomes " Roberto " ) , is reprinted here , as are bits and pieces of poems and essays that Lerner has published elsewhere . Other people 's stories figure prominently as well , such as the aforementioned woman 's revelation about her paternity , or a man discovering that his girlfriend faked her cancer ; there are photos of Mars and stills from Back to the Future . This gives the book the feel of a palimpsest , a conscious move ; at one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the beauty of a Ronald Reagan speech , written by someone else , that included unattributed quotations from a poem that was itself plagiarized : " a kind of palimpsestic plagiarism that moves through bodies and time , a collective song with no single origin , or whose origin has been erased -- the way a star , from our earthly perspective , is often survived by its own light . " <p> Although 10:04 is undoubtedly " metafictional , " it is not necessarily ( or only ) about " fiction " in the literary sense . Its metafiction is a vessel for explicitly discussing other realities , other ways in which human experience could manifest itself . While this is arguably true of all fiction , in 10:04 it takes on a specialized meaning . This metafiction does n't just invite us to inhabit another person , or consider life in an alternative universe ; instead , it presents us , at every turn , with new forks in the road , forcing us to consider what it means to take one path rather than another . In pondering @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world in the brontosaurus was real , rather than a palimpsest of other dinosaur bones ; or in which pigeons are members of the order Passeriformes rather than Columbiformes ; or in which art becomes garbage . To the extent that 10:04 is " about " anything , it is about the tensions between , and possibilities of , these different worlds and different futures . <p> The book 's weakest chapter is also , not surprisingly , its most solipsistic ; the narrator travels to a residency in Marfa , Texas , where he retreats into himself and works on anything other than his novel , which was originally supposed to be about a writer who fabricates correspondence with other , more famous writers . But then things take a poignant turn , and , following a traumatic , ketamine-fueled house party , he comes to a realization : " I decided to replace the book I 'd proposed with the book you 're reading now , a work that , like a poem , is neither fiction nor nonfiction , but a flickering between them ; I resolved to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , about fabricating the past , but into an actual present alive with multiple futures . @@2773904 <h> Pyramid blue spruce <h> Deciduous spreading tulip tree <h> Columnar cedars <h> Columnar conifers <h> SIDEBAR <p> Trees -- Shape and Form <p> Trees are important to everyone and there are endless varieties of different forms , shapes , textures and colours to choose from . Here 's a primer on some different shapes trees have which can help you in adding them as a design element into your landscape . <p> Columnar -- the name says it all these trees grow like a cylinder or column . Columnar trees grow straight and tall with branches uniform length from top to bottom great for a narrow space . Columnar cedar can be used as a single tree in the garden or placed next to each other to create a hedge . Deciduous examples are poplar or aspen . <p> Weeping -- the branches of a weeping tree droop down instead of reaching for the sky with graceful cascading foliage . Weeping trees add interest to the landscape such as weeping willow or birch for a large property or a weeping mulberry , an ornamental for a smaller @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ broad cone or pyramid shape with a wide base and narrow top . These trees require a wide base area . Blue Spruce is a good example of a conical shape tree . <p> Deciduous spreading -- this is what we might think of as standard tree growth . Branches are wide at the canopy and can be overwhelming for small properties . Maples , apples and oak are examples of deciduous spreading trees providing a wide shade canopy under which to sit . <p> Remember when selecting trees that you must consider their mature size not the size they are when you purchase them . Make sure you read the plant tag and place them in a location needed for their mature height and width . If you do n't you 'll have to remove them later on when they have outgrown the space which can be costly and difficult . Alternatively , you can look for dwarf species of trees which are slower growing and smaller in size . <p> " Tree planting is always a utopian enterprise , it seems to me , a wager on a future @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- Michael Pollan , Second Nature : A Gardener 's Education <p> Trees are an integral part of the Muskoka landscape but how many of us really think about the value of trees -- how they add to the landscape , what they provide for birds , wildlife and ourselves , and the fact that they are a necessary component of our eco-systems . In fact did you know that one tree can be a complete eco-system supporting a variety of wildlife ? Trees provide birds , wildlife and ourselves with habitat , and shelter . As a food source as we enjoy nuts , berries and fruit like apples , peaches , pears and plums grown on trees to sustain us in summer and throughout the year . We use wood from trees grown in sustainable forests for building material for our homes , furniture , outdoor decks and garden structures . In many parts of the world wood is used as fuel for cooking and heating and wood pulp supplies us with paper products . Trees also have a spiritual place in the world , majestic , regal , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ longevity symbolizing special moments in people 's lives . Trees are needed for photosynthesis , one of the most important processes in nature . In photosynthesis , carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) enters tree leaves and is replaced by oxygen released back into our environment during the day allowing us to breathe . At night this process is reversed with leaves taking in oxygen releasing back CO2 helping to absorb pollution . According to the U.S. Department of Agriculture one acre of forest absorbs six tons of CO2 and injects four tons of oxygen back into the environment enough to meet the annual needs of 18 people . Without photosynthesis plants could n't grow nor could we exist . The high tree canopy is important for deflecting sunlight cooling our homes in the heat of summer providing energy savings and allowing shade loving plants to take root and flourish in the shaded ground below . The canopy shields us from rain , sleet and hail . The roots anchor the soil , fight erosion absorbing rainwater reducing runoff and helping to control floods . Trees can also act as windbreaks providing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rate is the great deforestation of planet earth . As a result of loss of biodiversity , habitat and food sources millions of animals , birds and insects are dying . The negative results affect not only birds and animals but ourselves as deforestation leads to soil erosion , destruction of water sources , floods , mudslides and droughts resulting in loss of homes , arable , fertile farmland and lack of water . There is much denial about deforestation as we tend only to look at things in isolation , we neglect that we 're all part of complicated interconnected ecosystems and when one component of the system is removed it causes a chain reaction like a set of dominoes or house of cards being knocked over . We do n't usually think about this when just one tree is cut down , but cut down many trees and the problems are revealed . Every second an area the size of a soccer field of trees is cut down somewhere in the world . I have seen destruction through deforestation in Kenya and have known friends who have lost their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cutting down trees . But it 's not too late . If we realize the harm we are doing to all who rely on the natural world and trees we can rectify the situation by planting more trees . Wangari Maathai , 2004 Nobel Peace Prize winner from Kenya recognized what was happening in Kenya and other developing countries around the globe , but also that developed countries were to blame too . She began simply by educating women who used trees for firewood and building materials on the need to plant trees to replace the ones they were using improving their local environment . When women understood the negative effect they were causing they began planting trees and The Green Belt Movement was started . Just prior to her death , Wangari initiated the Billion Tree Campaign encouraging people worldwide to plant a billion trees to help combat negative effects of deforestation on climate change . If we all refrained from cutting healthy trees down and planted one or two trees during our lifetime imagine the impact we could have on the planet . Deforestation is a real issue , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our own community does n't mean we do n't feel effects of it . This quote from Jim Robbins from his book , The Man Who Planted Trees : Lost Groves , Champion Trees , and an Urgent Plan to Save the Planet , says it all : " Planting trees , I myself thought for a long time , was a feel-good thing , a nice but feeble response to our litany of modern-day environmental problems . In the last few years , though , as I have read many dozens of articles and books and interviewed scientists here and abroad , my thinking on the issue has changed . Planting trees may be the single most important ecotechnology that we have to put the broken pieces of our planet back together . "

@@2776063 <p> Cartoon creatures populate the books Naomi Klein reads most often these days . <p> " All the books are about animals , " the Toronto author says of the bedtime stories she shares with her 2-year-old son , Toma . <p> " And at a certain point it just struck me that so many of the books . . . were about animals that are facing ( climate related ) extinction crises and that he may never see some of the animals he 's reading about . " <p> Still , the species that most concerns Klein is her own . <p> And like a conclusive majority of climate scientists , she sees man-made , carbon-based global warming as settled fact and an immediate threat to human civilization . <p> In her own book , This Changes Everything , Capitalism vs The Climate , Klein advances this existential conundrum : <p> Capitalism , as it 's conceived and conducted today ; capitalism that relies on globalization , unbridled consumerism , deregulation and perpetual economic expansion , is irreconcilable with a livable climate . <p> And since humans @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carbon-stoked climate , she argues , capitalism will have to change . <p> The Star spoke with Klein about her new book -- to be published Sept. 20 by Knopf Canada . <p> Following is an edited version of the conversation . <p> You were once in denial about climate change . You did n't deny the science , but like so many others you turned cringingly away from the worsening news . What made you change your mind to take a hard look and write a book about it ? <p> What changed for me was hearing the argument for the existence of a climate debt , which is the idea that in order to address the crisis . . . which was created by the wealthiest countries in the world and is being felt most acutely by some of the poorest countries in the world , there needs to be a process of redress . <p> Core inequalities need to be tackled through redistribution of wealth and technology . And this was explained to me as a chance to heal the world ; to heal some of the deepest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ suddenly saw that though this crisis continues to be existentially terrifying , it could also be a catalyst for really inspiring change and social justice . <p> I want to talk about those changes , but again , many people continue to ignore or outright deny climate change . And this denial is based largely on the ( oil-interest inspired ) belief that the science is still unsettled . How would you suggest that manufactured scientific controversy be surmounted ? <p> In the book I make a distinction between hard denial and soft denial . The hard denial is it 's not happening -- the science is wrong , it 's a conspiracy . That 's actually quite a small percentage of the population , particularly in Canada . Overwhelmingly , Canadians believe that climate change is real . <p> Where the impact of the oil lobby and misinformation campaigns have been very effective ( is in feeding ) that soft denial of " I ca n't bear to look . " <p> I think that state of not looking , in Canada , is very much about this really successful @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our economy will collapse . Everything good that is happening in the country is because of the oil boom . <p> In Canada , ( that ) has been a much more effective way of preventing us from focusing on the climate crisis than outright scientific denial of the facts . <p> And that 's why I hope the book will play a constructive role in this debate because I am outlining an economic future that is not collapse , that is not grim , that is in fact , I hope , more exciting and inspiring than the economic choices we are being presented as Canadians . <p> I think just highlighting other economic alternatives . . . success stories where that false dichotomy has been rejected , opens up the possibilities to look . <p> There is certainly big money behind the most important player in this debate , the United States , and that money reaches deep into the halls of Congress where people appear to be paid to deny the existence of the problem . But you argue in the book that big money is n't the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ generating that big money that 's at fault . You argue that , in fact , capitalism as it 's currently practiced is incompatible with saving the planet . Can you explain that ? <p> Well , I think that in terms of responding decisively to climate change I do think that big money is at the heart of the suppression of that response . <p> Both by . . . bribing politicians and serving as ( an election-campaign ) disciplinary force for politicians -- you get the money if you do the right thing . But if you do n't do the right thing from the perspective of the oil companies then that same money is used to attack you in television ads and so on . <p> But in terms of what you 're saying about what the book argues about capitalism , I think the resistance by some of the most powerful players in the world . . . to a decisive response to climate change ( comes about ) because there is an understanding that we 've allowed the crisis to deepen to such an extent that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what science is telling us , then the cuts would be so deep that they would present a fundamental challenge to the logic of growth for growth 's sake , which is the logic of the core of our economic system . <p> That does n't mean there ca n't be parts of our economy that grow , there can and must be . . . in order to respond to climate change . But as that happens , we also need to contract those parts of our economy that are destabilizing the climate and are just fuelling mindless consumption . <p> Some of the aspects of that perpetual growth economy are rampant consumerism , globalization and deregulation . . . those seem ingrained and backed by irresistible ( business ) forces . How do you convince enough of those people who are more concerned with a bigger TV and the newest logo-bearing product that they can rise up and face those powerful interests ? <p> I think that is the key question and it is to me the greatest crime of neo-liberal ideological messaging ; that so many of us @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gratification-seeking units . <p> We ( believe ) we 're so unidimensional in our values and desires that we are incapable of responding collectively to an existential crisis and incapable of acting collectively for a greater good . <p> Well , we know that historically , this has not been the case . We know that in the midst of the Great Depression we came together and built some of the social programs we 're proudest of . We know the same is true of wartime rationing . There 's an incredible statistic I have in the book about how use of public transit in Canada increased by 95 per cent during the Second World War . <p> So really , what we 're saying when we say we ca n't do this -- and this is what we 're told again and again , we ca n't do this , there is something wrong with us , we 're too selfish , we 're too greedy -- is that this idea of the " we " of who we are has changed . <p> Maybe our grandparents could do things @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . . . ( but ) it 's almost like we think we 're a different species from our grandparents .

The NHL , while not single-handedly a significant climate villain , does require a lot of increasingly scarce resources to operate . For example , a single sheet of rink ice requires 12,500 gallons of water . <p> Often enough , major organizations produce white papers or issue press releases as a way of signalling a commitment to environmental causes , paying lip service in order to mark themselves out as good corporate citizens without actually doing much of anything . <p> In the case @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ clear in the Sustainability Report 's introductory section that the league is committed to pushing good environmental policy mainly because it affects ice hockey 's bottom line . @@2777370 <h> Michael Den Tandt : Justin Trudeau 's weak spot only made worse by his lack of a platform <p> For months Justin Trudeau has had his mitts in the air , deflecting right and left hooks from seasoned combatants who would love to connect solidly with his jaw , just once . The Liberal leader 's main point of vulnerability , they believe , is his lack of substance -- which includes questions about his experience and judgment , but is mainly exemplified by his lack of a platform . <p> On Friday evening in Markham , Ont. , poor Justin Trudeau took the stage to address the federal Liberals ' Ontario supporters trailing bad news behind him like a bunch of tin cans on a string . The Liberals ' average polling number in Ontario fell four whole points in August , according to the polling aggregator threehundredeight.com . That shrinks their lead over Stephen Harper 's Conservatives to a precarious four points in the province -- and just seven points nationwide . Some serious morale-boosting was obviously in order . <p> I kid . Mr. @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Friday night capacity crowd at the Hilton convention centre seemed to be in high spirits indeed . And why not ? The Liberals are back on the cusp of power barely having broken a sweat . Until the campaign gets going next year , they may face no threat more dire than complacency . <p> Mr. Trudeau was all over that . " I 've ... been asked a lot about the Liberal party 's chances of forming the next government , " he said . " Well I certainly hope you know the answer to that question . " ( He meant it 's " yes . " ) <p> This raises an obvious question : Why have n't the Liberals closed that chink in their leader 's armour ? <p> Tom Mulcair , the NDP leader , promises to issue his own platform ( The Orange Book ? The Crush Manifesto ? ) within weeks . The Harper government on Friday announced a half-billion-dollar cut in small-businesses employment insurance premiums , the strongest signal yet that its 2015 campaign is already on . Trudeau , meantime , continues to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ book , both of which will be ready by the time the writ is dropped . He began sketching the broad outlines of his direction the day he announced his run for the Liberal leadership in 2012 . He has called it Wilfrid Laurier liberalism ; but essentially it 's a quite recognizeable marriage of progressive social ideals with conservative economic orthodoxy . <p> More recently Trudeau has framed a skeleton of five core economic platform principles . He articulated these in April in a speech to the Vancouver Board of Trade . Friday , in London , Ont. , he ticked them off again . They are education and training ; infrastructure development , both conventional ( roads and bridges ) and digital ; free trade and enhanced foreign direct investment ; sustainable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there , in other words , that a small-c conservative could n't live with or even embrace . As I have written before , the Liberals ' strategic objective here is simple appropriation ; to walk into the Tories ' mantle of economic rectitude like a poncho hanging on a clothesline , shrug it on , and just keep walking . Married to left-of-centre social policy -- marijuana legalization and foursquare support for women 's reproductive rights , to mention two examples -- this can be politically potent . <p> That said , there are enormous gaps in the five principles , and they grow more apparent as time passes . Because significant income redistribution is both politically toxic and difficult to enforce , education has to be the critical piece in ameliorating income inequality , Trudeau 's core stated objective . But education is a provincial responsibility . How does Ottawa introduce reform , in any way that will make a dent , without big-footing provincial powers ? And what would those reforms look like ? We still have no idea . <p> With respect to infrastructure spending , how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what ? How will it be costed and paid for ? Liberalized trade , we have seen for years , is a Conservative strength , culminating in the pending Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement with Europe , and continuing talks with Japan and India , among many others . What can the Liberals offer here that the Conservatives have n't , or wo n't ? Trudeau has n't said . <p> Resource development -- from pipelines to climate change to relations with First Nations and everything in between -- is both critically important to the country 's economic future , and a hugely daunting web of interconnected problems . We know Trudeau thinks there should be a price on carbon . How ? At what cost , and who will pay ? Everybody favours innovation . But how would the Liberals seek to stimulate it , without falling into new corporate welfare and billion-dollar boondoggles ? <p> The larger policy question is simply this : Trudeau has cast a narrative , which he repeated Sunday in an interview with CBC radio host Michael Enright , that the Liberal party in power @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has said , is wholesale rebuilding , from the ground up . But there are few indications the rest of his party is as enthusiastic about jettisoning the past . If he 's serious about starting fresh , he will need to bring traditional Liberals -- who still think the long-gun registry , universal day care , the Kelowna Accord and a carbon tax were terrific ideas -- along with him . He is running out of time in which to do so . <p> Politically , meantime , the risk in waiting grows with each passing day . As long as there 's no Liberal platform , the focus rests on Trudeau personally . That 's fine for his party when he 's articulate and persuasive , as he was in the interview Sunday with Enright . It 'll be far less so when he slips up , which he inevitably will , just based on the scrutiny he receives , the expectations he 's raised about his own approachability and openness , and the odds . <p> To leave important policy questions unanswered , even as his rivals flesh @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to shrapnel he could otherwise avoid . Should he allow this to continue much longer , it will be his first , big strategic mistake .
